These are historical reported windows, not observed robot exposure, paid worker-hours, availability or accepted service. Anonymous cases remain distinct from the named government case; S-2 asset title remains unknown.

### Focused follow-up: Shared staffing and complete competing work

Authoritative analysis: [research/shared_workforce.md](research/shared_workforce.md).

Editions 0.35–0.37 · synthetic execution and decision challenge, not empirical deployment economics.

The [hospital economic audit](research/field_support_denominators.md#hospital-economic-claims-edition-034) made the missing organizational bridge explicit: released minutes do not identify paid staffing that can disappear. The new [shared-workforce contract](model/SHARED_WORKFORCE.md) executes that bridge conditionally. A single paid site crew must cover local robot attention, inventory jobs, robot handling and manual cleaning. A notice policy can reduce a future commitment; actual funds buy extra capacity; both service obligations and every payer remain visible.

The first decision is whether the whole workload is feasible. The second is whether its total resource and payment consequences support the deployment. A favorable answer on one staffing line cannot answer both questions.

#### What changes the choice

The fixed-crew and notice cases both deliver all 4,000 cleaning zones and 160 separate inventory jobs over eight months. Under the declared August notice taking effect in October, regular staffing cash falls from $32,000 to $22,000. Four months of notice instead retain $26,000. This is an executed synthetic commitment change, not a dollar value assigned to released minutes, a measured employment result or proof that a real contract permits the reduction. Transition costs beyond retained notice-period staffing are unknown.

The benefit is still conditional on other work. Doubling inventory demand in the last four months requires 1,240 extra paid crew minutes per affected month. Funding them costs $4,960 over the window and preserves complete service. With no extra capacity, the same regular staffing outlay buys only 2,000 cleaning zones and 232 of 240 required inventory jobs; only four months meet the complete obligation. Its smaller material/cash bill cannot support a savings claim. At an extra-capacity ceiling of 1,239 minutes, all inventory jobs are completed but cleaning falls to 3,998 zones. One missing minute per affected month is enough to fail the complete-service test.

The human comparator changes the strategic interpretation. The original digital-human path needs $7,680 of extra capacity to cover the same 4,000 zones and 160 jobs. A stronger assumed dispatch path reduces cleaning work sufficiently to fit the existing paid crew, leaving inventory work unchanged. It pays $32,000 of regular staffing and no extra capacity. Its bounded external resource cash is $43,900, compared with $223,289.36 for the robot notice case. The $10,000 staffing reduction therefore does not make the robot case the lower-cash alternative under these premises. Opening assets, closing liabilities/receivables, transition costs and terminal value remain outside any full lifecycle comparison; no real technology ranking or ROI follows.

#### Shared work and shared money must both be real

Inventory work is no longer merely an unavailable-capacity reservation. It has required job IDs, actual completed/failed work, a rate, an independently funded buyer and dated invoices. With the inventory buyer unfunded, all 160 jobs can still be delivered from the operator's existing capital, while no receipts arrive: $9,600 remains uncollected, of which $8,400 is already due at the window end. The funded-buyer case still has a $1,200 future-due receivable. Neither a completed job nor an invoice becomes cash automatically.

Local robot attention and handling compete with manual cleaning and inventory work for the same identified crew. Professional model engineering, demonstrations, support and integration retain their distinct resources and invoices. Combining their labels into a notional shared labor pool would fabricate substitutability. Conversely, actual legal consolidation matters for funding: customer and operator logical capital share an account in the licensed profile. A zero allocation to one logical role does not exhaust the other role's cash. The adverse funding tests respect that identity.

The notice rule uses prior completed work and supported units, then waits through its declared delay. It cannot anticipate the later inventory surge or retroactively remove staff from earlier months. Once issued, a later shortfall does not cancel the modeled notice; the service ledger exposes the resulting extra-capacity need or missed work. A real deployment requires its own employment/contract authority, transition cost, working schedule and recovery options before this premise can support a decision.

The thin model-role funding world also passes the early notice condition but fails the last month's complete obligation. Later support leaves fewer operating robots, and the operator cannot prepay the extra crew minutes. A valid historical trigger therefore does not guarantee sustained staffing feasibility: maintenance funding, fallback liquidity and the staffing commitment must be assessed together over the intended horizon.

#### Consequences for the three decision packs

For hospital logistics, qualify the entire request cohort and all retained handling, observation, dispatch, repair and fallback work before financing deployment from staffing savings. For utility inspection, include the residual human route and other paid crew duties. For automotive workcells, include feed, inspection, recovery and the crew's competing production obligations. These are measurement and accounting requirements; the cleaning/inventory rates, notice terms, prices and currencies do not transfer into those cells.

Proceed with a bounded comparison only when the required service, actual shared resource identity and funding can be specified. Wait when the business case relies on an unverified staffing transition or unpriced fallback. Reject a lower-cost comparison that misses required work, spends the same worker time twice, counts future receipts as available cash, or treats a proposed staffing cut as a measured payroll result. Keep the improved human/specialist alternative in the same decision set.

The initial edition 0.35 of [the complete export](model/results/shared_workforce.json) contained nine replayable worlds with notice events, minute bookings, distinct service totals, role accounts and all invoices. It deepens DE-CF01 rather than inventing a new challenge family: fourteen families then contained 196 bounded worlds. These are independent comparisons, not one simultaneously feasible six-market portfolio. Full empirical cost, rights, organization and broad cross-module integration remain partial.

#### Prospective purchase reserve, edition 0.37

The [historical workforce audit](research/field_support_denominators.md#employment-operating-windows-and-fallback-edition-036) identified the need for jointly staffed receiving duties and fallback. This extension tests one funding response inside the same execution. It does not turn a source's manual contingency into available workers. Nine additional worlds bring this package to eighteen; all nine original worlds retain their numerical outcomes. The same fourteen campaign challenge families now contain 205 bounded worlds, not one simultaneously feasible portfolio.

**The decision is a prospective operator cash floor on optional hardware purchase.** The independent OEM funds manufacture; the operator pays for delivered hardware and site crew time. At each shipment, the policy compares actual operator cash remaining after that purchase group with a fixed, declared floor. The activation month is also fixed at opening. Neither future workload nor future receipts enter this decision. The policy keeps existing support, commissioning, mandatory liabilities and overtime ordering intact. These payments can consume retained cash: the floor is a purchase rule, not escrow, an expense, a worker guarantee or an optimized liquidity requirement. Refusal counts describe repeated decisions and can include already-unaffordable quotes; inspect the matched zero-floor case to identify the policy effect.

The constrained-site comparison starts with $80,000 in the legally combined customer/operator account, the existing two robots and a later rise from twenty to forty inventory jobs per month. With no floor, one new robot is purchased in November and only commissioned in February. That last month's extra crew booking cannot be prepaid: only seven months satisfy both services, with 3,530 of 4,000 cleaning zones and 238 of 240 inventory jobs delivered. A declared $20,000 purchase floor refuses the deliveries, retains the existing two-robot fleet and funds $4,480 of extra crew time. It delivers all 4,000 zones and 240 jobs. Regular staffing remains $22,000 in both cases; there is no retroactive notice reversal or free replacement staff.

The operator's protection has a counterparty consequence. Both worlds already manufactured two units for $72,000. Under the reserve they remain finished OEM stock; the manufacture is not refunded or removed from resource cost. OEM closing cash is $128,000 rather than $187,900 after one sale and its shipment cost. Operator closing cash is $79,792.80 rather than $1,154.85. Purchase fees, OEM resource payments and retained assets are separate; no reserve amount is added to the consolidated bill.

A second case tests the previously thin model-led operator. A $40,000 floor active before the August purchase retains two supported robots and complete service. Starting the same policy in September cannot reverse the earlier acquisition or the binding staffing notice: the final service failure remains. The first purchase leaves exactly $38,418.16 available. A floor at that value admits it; one cent more refuses it and changes the later outcome. This is an exact test of the declared admission rule, not an empirically sufficient reserve or a search for the optimal policy. Existing obligations can still consume liquidity after admission.

Cash and capacity must both exist. Removing extra crew availability from the $20,000-reserve surge case leaves only four complete months, 2,000 zones and 236 inventory jobs, despite more than $55,000 remaining in the operator account. Conversely, an independent model supplier with no initial funding remains unable to qualify/support robots even while the operator holds cash. The paid human fallback covers that case under the declared overtime contract; no money is silently transferred between legal actors. These worlds assume qualified extra crew can respond within the current month up to its ceiling, with full prepayment. They establish neither actual availability nor a hiring, training or response-time entitlement.

A very high floor can reject useful expansion. With only one opening robot, admitting the two new units produces twenty supported robot-months and permits a later staffing notice, retaining $26,000 of regular staffing. A deliberately excessive $1 million floor leaves eight supported robot-months, more human cleaning and $32,000 of staffing. Both deliver complete service; the blocked-investment case still has lower bounded resource cash. The lost staffing option therefore does not establish that the investment would have earned a return.

The comparator remains consequential. The stronger assumed human-dispatch path covers the same 4,000-zone/240-job surge, paying $32,000 regular staffing and $9,600 extra capacity, with $53,500 total external resource cash. The reserve-protected robot case pays less crew cash but $165,773.92 total external resource cash. Opening hardware, terminal stock, future claims and unmodeled transition costs prevent a lifecycle or ROI ranking. The assumed human improvement also requires empirical qualification; it is not observed AI performance.

For the three decision packs, require a payer-specific budget and actual qualified response capacity, preserve the service receiving reassigned labor, and test late action and counterparty costs. Do not fund a hospital, utility or workcell decision with these cleaning rates or reserve values. The next campaign priority is the competition/entry/recovery interface and stronger digital-human alternatives across the six cells, not another series of hospital-specific reserve variants.

#### Paid crews and actual customer awards, edition 0.41

The [joint crew execution](model/results/staffed_renewals.json) now connects the staffing decision to actual recurring customer awards, company entry and separate operating accounts. It is one physical/Treasury execution. The existing architecture/resource workforce fixture and the asset-recovery fixture remain separate; their results are not added. Thirteen new worlds deepen the existing fourteen challenge families to **241 bounded worlds**. No empirical coefficient or staffing ratio enters this extension.

##### The offer must fit the same crew that does the work

Each operating company has a unique qualified crew and legal payer. Regular staffing is a dated paid commitment. Before awards, each crew reserves local robot attention and its separately due inventory jobs. Remaining minutes support robot handling and human cleaning. The current robot cohort, ramp and digital-human work rate remain those of the existing calendar model. Raw robot output and the original human-capacity scalar cannot both consume the same minutes.

The order matters. A staffing notice uses only the prior completed month and supported fleet. Its reduction takes effect after the declared delay and remains binding through later shortfalls. A fixed optional overtime block is quoted in phase 1; mandatory bills and physical work occur before the full block is optionally paid in phase 5. The block targets the full fixed cleaning market before customer awards. Awards then consume feasible capacity and actual onboarding/switching funds; dispatch and new service invoices follow. Neither later receipts nor an expected award funds an earlier offer. Losing bids can leave prepaid capacity unused. This conventional policy is deliberately explicit and is not an optimized bid, staffing roster or legal employment rule.

Cleaning contracts are 50-zone lots in a fixed 500-zone monthly market. Inventory jobs have their own IDs, acceptance, deadline, payer and receipt lag; their quantities are never added to cleaning zones. Outside duties persist when cleaning contracts leave. Common ownership changes ultimate equity while operating cash, crew identities and liabilities remain separate. Another subsidiary's idle crew is not an authorized or qualified loan of staff.

##### Results that change the decision

The full control serves **4,000 cleaning zones and 160 inventory jobs** over eight months. A notice issued in month 3 becomes effective in month 5. Cutting the target from two blocks to one lowers its feasible offer below a complete 500-zone market; 50 zones move to the rival. Both services still finish, with **$6,000 less regular staffing cash** and changed customer assignment. This is a bounded synthetic staffing transition, not measured payroll removal.

When inventory demand rises from 20 to 45 jobs after notice, the smaller crew performs only **242 of 260 jobs**, while cleaning falls to **3,400 zones**. Four-month locked contracts worsen cleaning to **2,590 zones** because work remains assigned to the constrained firm. A rival with a larger paid human crew restores all **4,000 cleaning zones**, but still leaves the same **18 inventory jobs undone**. A headline showing only cleaning completion would miss the failure of the retained service.

Prepaying a 5,000-minute overtime block for the last three months restores all 260 jobs and 4,000 zones. It costs **$15,000** of overtime against the $6,000 regular-staff reduction. Total resource/transaction cash from every payer is **$141,647.74**. A stronger human-only comparator with three regular target blocks serves the identical surge and cleaning demand for **$103,300**, with no robot manufacture or lease. The **$38,347.74** window difference is conditional on these synthetic rates, unused capacity, funding and ending assets/claims; it is not a lifecycle return. In the original 160-job workload, the competent human-only comparison also finishes everything at **$87,300**, versus **$127,197.74** for the robot with the staffing cut.

Two exact boundaries expose discontinuities. Human-only target capacity is exactly sufficient at two 4,800-minute blocks; reducing each block by one minute moves a whole 50-zone lot to the rival while market service remains complete. For a one-block target, **$7,850 opening cash** leaves exactly **$4,800** after initial mandatory bills for a 4,800-minute overtime block. One cent less prevents prepayment, leaves **50 zones unserved** in month 0 and creates later switching/onboarding costs. Across the window, the short-funded case spends **$112,500** in resources versus **$109,700** at the exact threshold, despite less service. Future receipts do not repair the missed period.

A lower rival offer can also win work after the incumbent prepays capacity. The price difference must exceed the customer's switching resources; an exact tie preserves the incumbent. A self-review caught an initial test premise that only tied the old contract. The adverse offer now crosses that threshold and verifies paid unused capacity after lost awards.

##### Cash, scope and observation required

Staffing, overtime and outside receipts are already recorded in the enterprise ledger. The separate workforce view is a reconciliation, not another cost to add. All-payer resource/transaction cash includes outside customer switching bills and independently equals receipts of the modeled resource vendors. Company consideration, internal equipment payments, equity and loan principal are not added as resource consumption. Ending receivables, payables and physical assets remain visible; there is no residual valuation.

This advances the [labor-outcome gate](research/digital_labor_outcomes.md) without converting a survey answer into a staffing coefficient. The missing target observation is a dated roster, qualifications, retained duties, actual customer awards, notice/overtime obligations and every payer's cash through the same transition. A whole cleaning market, a viable operating company and complete retained work are distinct acceptance tests. The hospital, utility and automotive packs retain their own service units and rights; this cleaning contract supplies a mechanism challenge rather than a transfer of minutes or prices. Monthly divisible cleaning omits within-shift travel, sequencing and timed acceptance. Full six-cell portfolio feasibility, annual resource/parts composition, recovery, endogenous learning and empirical whole-system economics remain incomplete.

#### Qualified support, parts and binding staffing, edition 0.44

[The qualified-staffing execution](model/results/qualified_staffing.json) closes a specific interface left open by edition 0.41: annual provider support/integration and dated compatible parts now gate the same physical units that inform staffing notice, paid site work, company entry and actual customer awards. One physical loop and one Treasury execute all of them. This is a synthetic mechanism test, not a target staffing recommendation. The four architecture alternatives, asset-recovery and learning fixtures remain separate. Nine executions deepen existing challenge families to 250 bounded worlds; they are not nine additional empirical observations or a jointly feasible six-cell portfolio.

##### Forward coverage matters after a successful month

The unreserved control preserves the previous binding-staff-cut history: 4,000 accepted cleaning zones, 160 inventory jobs, the same cash, title and customer-award history. Its one robot needs 650 minutes of provider support each operating month in 2026. Four supported months need exactly 2,600 available minutes. These provider engineers are different resources from the two operating companies' qualified site crews. Integration uses another pool. An annual reservation elsewhere makes capacity unavailable without inventing another executed job, employee or expense.

A successful September (month 2) permits notice in October (month 3), reducing the target's paid crew from two blocks to one in December (month 5). **2,600 versus 2,599 available provider minutes** changes December support: 649 minutes left cannot admit a whole 650-minute booking. The staff cut remains binding. The target can cover only three 50-zone lots after its inventory duties; the rival can cover six. One lot is unassigned and **50 zones go unserved**, while all 160 inventory jobs finish. January capacity restores robot service but cannot restore December's missed output. Thus eight complete months become seven even though the earlier notice test was satisfied.

Keeping the original paid staffing through the same support shortage restores all 4,000 zones and 160 jobs for **$131,806.45** of all-payer resource/transaction cash. The cut-and-shortfall case spends **$132,856.45** for less service. Its $6,000 lower regular staffing is outweighed by $7,100 of onboarding/customer-switching resource payments, partly offset by $50 less service material use. The **$1,050 reversal** is a modeled consequence of actual awards and payers, not a valuation of missed work. A fixed 5,000-minute prospective overtime block also restores all service, but costs **$140,806.45** across the window; its $15,000 overtime outlay is already inside that figure. The declared whole-market offer policy buys some capacity that awards leave unused. It is not optimized fallback procurement.

The digital-improved human-only comparison completes the identical obligations for **$87,300**, versus **$127,197.74** in the fully supported robot/staff-cut control. Ending robot assets, receivables, claims and unmodeled lifecycle costs remain; the window difference is not ROI, payback or a demonstrated employment effect. A model that only values removed regular staffing would miss both the conventional alternative and the switching/fallback costs.

##### A received spare is not timely qualified service

A named actuator replacement is due in November (month 4). In one case its compatible spare is physically present but does not qualify until January's phase 4. Monthly support is admitted earlier, in phase 3; repair therefore completes in February (month 7). The successful September still triggered binding December notice. Retained human capacity covers November, but December and January each miss 50 cleaning zones. The final total is **3,900 zones**, all 160 inventory jobs, and six complete months. Qualification does not backfill earlier service, and failed support consumes neither the spare nor annual minutes.

If the manufacturing board never qualifies, no robot is manufactured and no staffing notice is issued. The fully retained human crew completes service. Consigned unissued inventory is not charged an invented supplier-financing cost: the current model prices parts once inside paid manufacturing/support bundles. This assumption remains a real commercial evidence gap.

Giving both operating companies the same robot access does not double the shared provider pool. Two robots compete under the existing deterministic admission order for the same 2,600 first-year support minutes; four bookings fail. Separate site crews and legal cash remain distinct even after common equity ownership. More procured robots therefore do not guarantee more complete service. This is an allocation-policy witness, not an optimized fleet allocation or a measured exclusive technology advantage.

##### Target decision and broader scope

Before calling a paid block removable, match its notice horizon to a dated forward plan: qualified parts and replacement timing, provider entitlement and allocated capacity, retained site duties, funded fallback, actual customer contract commitments and each payer. A successful prior month is necessary under this declared rule but insufficient for future coverage. A review must also preserve the option to retain staffing, defer procurement or improve the human workflow.

The three decision packs carry this forward-coverage gate without transferring cleaning minutes, prices or staffing blocks. Hospital delivery requires timely repair coverage and a complete qualified handling/fallback roster; automotive workcells require compatible parts, line qualification and retained technician work; utility inspection requires accepted information, the intervention it changes, site work, analyst duties and support rights. Construction installation additionally needs trade/permit competence; warehouse tote service needs safe handling, handoffs and residual inventory duties. None has an empirical payroll coefficient supplied by this fixture. Substantial H contributes dated coordination and a better human baseline; I/J/K retain embodiment, qualified supply and model/support rights rather than treating software capability as support entitlement.

The source access limits from edition 0.43 remain closed. No new public-source claim or numerical calibration was introduced. Current operator accepted-service/maintenance decisions, actual paid work and all-payer costs remain missing; this integration resolves a model interface, not that empirical dependency.

#### Intelligence roles, staffing payers and actual awards, edition 0.45

[The architecture/award execution](model/results/architecture_awards.json) brings the four existing intelligence arrangements into the same entry, customer-award, qualified-resource and paid-crew history. Each complete case configures one target service business and a separate human-led rival. The target's customer company, physical service operator, OEM, model supplier and integrator have explicit roles; roles may share a legal account only where the selected arrangement says so. This is one physical loop and Treasury, not a sum of the earlier architecture and competition results. The default hybrid keeps separate declared operating-company offers after common ownership; it is not independent competition or optimized group pricing.

##### The employer, asset owner and customer-contract owner can differ

In the model-led operator arrangement the customer company retains its end-market contracts, service receipts and ultimate-equity register. The separately contracted model operator employs the target's paid crew, pays its staffing, overtime, site/software and operating costs, and buys and owns its robot. The operator also occupies the model-supplier/integrator roles. Acquiring the customer company does not acquire that separate operator's robot, pool its cash or transfer its staff. The rival funds its own qualified human crew. Customer-contract cash rows and service-operator cash therefore refer to different legal accounts.

The target service contract pays a declared amount per accepted cleaning zone after delivery and includes the retained inventory duties. No separate inventory fee is assumed. Same-entity fees are eliminated; external model, integration and operator prices remain transfers within the modeled production boundary. Their actual payment can matter for liquidity without becoming another resource expense. All-payer resource/transaction cash independently equals the modeled outside resource suppliers' receipts, including the architecture resource supplier once. The customer company's paid labor and an outsourced operator's paid labor must not be confused with one another or with released task time.

##### Equal capital can leave service unfunded

All nine displayed worlds have **$600,000 of declared logical-role opening capital**, and the same total cash across all modeled accounts. With $200,000 at the customer company, $200,000 at the integrated model operator and $200,000 at the hardware OEM, the model-operator case completes **4,000 cleaning zones and 160 inventory jobs**. It has six supported robot-months and eight complete service months.

Move the operator's $200,000 to the customer while keeping total capital unchanged. The operator cannot pay its initial mandatory crew and site obligations. Future service fees cannot fund earlier work that has not occurred, and an acquisition of the customer company creates no automatic operator funding transfer. The human rival completes **2,400 of 4,000 zones**, but all **160 inventory jobs remain undone**. The operator ends with **$40,400 of due unpaid obligations**, while the customer still holds its $400,000. The OEM has paid **$36,000** to manufacture a unit which remains its unsold asset. Cash availability in the production system is not cash availability at the party responsible for delivery.

The matched no-robot version fails in exactly the same way: 2,400 zones, zero inventory jobs and $40,400 of unpaid operator obligations. Its resource outlay is $36,000 lower because no robot is manufactured. That paired result identifies a funding/contract failure rather than a robot-capability effect. Lower cash spending alongside missing service and unpaid bills is not a valid saving.

When properly funded, both the licensed human-only and outsourced-operator human-only comparisons complete the identical work for **$87,300** of all-payer resource/transaction cash. The four fully funded robot arrangements complete the work for **$151,097.74** (integrated OEM), **$148,847.72** (licensed independent), **$170,197.72** (open adapted) and **$149,002.76** (model-led operator). These figures reflect catalog assumptions, paid manufacture, adaptation/qualification conventions, retained staffing, actual awards and ending assets/claims. They do not rank real architectures, estimate lab economics or establish lifecycle ROI.

##### Qualified work and fees are different constraints

The open-adapted target requires 300 engineering hours for its declared first admission. A 299-hour monthly budget prevents qualified robot service throughout this window, despite paid manufacture and purchase. Its retained human crew still supplies all 4,000 zones and 160 inventory jobs; no staffing cut is triggered. The window resource cash is $123,400, including the unused robot's manufacture and shipping. A targeted exact-boundary check at 300 hours admits the unit. This is a synthetic capacity gate, not observed model-development labor, a physical experiment or a learning curve.

A separate matched check doubles the funded model-operator service fee while preserving actual awards, accepted work and resource cash. Customer and operator cash balances change; the fee is not added again as resource consumption. This invariance is conditional on the tested funding remaining sufficient. Prices can still change feasibility when a payer constraint binds. Annual support refusals also roll back contingent architecture fees and preserve replacement stock; qualified parts, engineering, provider support and site crews do not become interchangeable capacity pools.

##### What this closes and what remains

The integration makes substantial H/K role economics affect real paid work and awards, with I/J supply/qualification and F/G company/asset boundaries retained. The three service-specific decision packs and six cell reviews require the actual employer, hardware owner, contracting customer, support/qualification payer, fee timing, retained duties and a competent human alternative. None imports the cleaning fixture's prices, work rates or architecture ranking.

One architectural target and a human-led rival are implemented. Two architectural fleets with separate profile mappings, shared adaptation/data rights, costed unsuccessful model-development trials, endogenous learning and asset recovery still require compatible contracts. The current architecture convention charges initial model work at the first successful admission; it does not measure or reconstruct earlier failed R&D. Actual operator quotes, rosters, accepted-service ledgers, rights, provider margins and all-payer lifecycle costs remain unknown. The fixed share price is a scenario term, not comparable company valuation across differently funded or outsourced organizations. No new primary-source claim or empirical coefficient was added, and closed source routes were not retried.

### Focused follow-up: Monthly commercial funding and title

Authoritative analysis: [research/monthly_commercial_finance.md](research/monthly_commercial_finance.md).

The commercial arrangement can change whether a technically identical plan is executed, because different actors must settle different obligations at different dates. The new [monthly contract model](model/MONTHLY_COMMERCIAL.md) isolates this mechanism using the accepted Treasury and physical-flow engines. It compares outright sale, independent lease and provider-owned availability service. All amounts and operating terms are **synthetic accounting witnesses**, not observed prices or deployment economics.

This distinction matters for the provider/customer workstreams and Workstream K's value-capture question. A model or operator may have a positive allocated lifetime result and still lack the cash needed for the next manufacturing, installation or support step. The K architecture model is a separate conditional lifetime comparison; these monthly contracts do not yet import its adaptation costs, model fees, support deltas or qualified configurations. No claim of joint financeability follows by placing their tables next to one another.

#### What the funded comparison isolates

Each world begins with the same requested manufacture, qualified-component access, site demand, shipping and commissioning slots, and support requirements. There is one month-0 paid start, month-1 acceptance/shipment, month-2 installation, then a quarter first month of output followed by two full months. Each funded world supplies 225 accepted units over the five-month horizon and incurs $37,400 of included physical vendor outlays. The provider pays manufacture and transport in every world. The sale customer or lessor buys the hardware; customers pay separate installation/support in sale and lease. The service provider retains the robot and pays those operating costs.

The price and fees determine who holds the cash, asset and obligations. In the synthetic reference, the $60,000 acquisition price is paid once. An independent lessor pays it; the operator does not also buy the robot. Lease payments are $10,000 per installed robot-month, including commissioning without proration. Provider service charges $10,000 per actually supported robot-month and collects one month later. The final service fee remains a receivable at the horizon, rather than being counted as cash already collected. None of these fees estimates an actual market price, target return or model-company margin.

Under ample funding, increasing the hardware price by $5,000 transfers $5,000 from purchaser to provider without changing physical resource use or output. That invariance is conditional: if the higher price crosses the purchaser's cash limit, shipment fails and realized output falls. Prices therefore redistribute cash directly and can alter physical activity indirectly through financeability. A comparison that forces output to remain equal after that failure would hide the mechanism it is supposed to explain.

#### What the adverse cases change

A provider opening with $10,000 cannot pay the $36,000 start. A willing customer with enough money to buy the eventual robot does not create a current receipt: no unit is manufactured, no purchase is collected and no output appears. Conversely, after a funded start has produced an accepted unit, a settled shipment receipt may fund a later start under the explicitly ordered phase-5 policy. The engine does not borrow from the future to manufacture the unit that must be sold first.

An independent lessor with one cent less than the purchase price cannot buy the robot. The provider retains the finished asset; transport is not charged, title does not change and output stays zero. If the purchase would settle but transport cannot be paid even afterward, the grouped shipment fails without committing the purchase receipt. This avoids a financially paid transfer that silently disappears from the physical ledger.

A lease customer with $20,000 can pay the first $10,000 rent and $1,100 commissioning/support package, leaving $8,900. The next $10,000 rent cannot settle. The full unpaid fee remains outstanding, support stops and the robot remains installed with the lessor as title owner. At the end of this five-month case, $20,000 rent is unpaid and output is only the first 25 units. The installed-robot count alone would conceal that production stopped. A separate test supplies later customer cash, pays arrears first, and resumes ordinary support without recharging installation or applying another commissioning ramp.

Provider service changes the party bearing the operating gap. With opening provider cash of $37,200 and a two-month collection lag, manufacture, shipment and commissioning exhaust available cash after the first 25 output units. The next month's support cannot be paid. The first service receipt arrives in month 4, funds actual support, and permits 100 units. Total output is 125, with no service invoice generated for the unsupported month. A receivable can describe future cash without supplying current maintenance.

Refundable deposits expose a different risk. A restricted $30,000 deposit added to $10,000 of provider cash still leaves only $10,000 spendable, so the start is blocked and cancellation can refund the deposit. If the same deposit is spendable, the provider can pay the $36,000 start. Cancellation the next month leaves an accepted, unsold unit, $4,000 cash and a $30,000 refund liability. Manufacturing expenditure is not reversed. An explicit later receipt can finance the refund; there is no automatic write-off or recovery value.

#### Interpretation and next evidence

This result supports a conditional proposition: changing asset ownership or payment terms can overcome one actor's funding constraint while moving exposure to another. It does not prove that leasing or service expands aggregate finance, lowers complete lifecycle resource costs or produces a superior investment. The lessor's funds are explicit opening money, not costless bank credit; its acquisition is a transfer rather than another manufactured robot. All arrangements compete for the same physical inputs and support capacity.

The remaining empirical question is which actual contract terms, billing milestones, qualification obligations, capital costs and service responsibilities govern a matched deployment. The remaining model question is whether an operating entrant can acquire customers and meet due enterprise obligations while drawing from the same robot, support and customer-demand pools as the incumbent. That requires a common demand ledger and company funding schedules. The present synthetic contract extension advances this question while leaving the annual fleet, separate entrant reference and K lifetime architectures explicitly distinct.

### Focused follow-up: A shared entrant market and funded execution

Authoritative analysis: [research/shared_enterprise_finance.md](research/shared_enterprise_finance.md).

The new shared-market experiment connects the customer-side entry thesis to actual commissioned robots, recurring customer assignments and paid enterprise obligations. It advances the earlier separate entrant illustration without treating its hypothetical capacity as another available fleet. Both operators use one technology and compete for one physical pool. All prices, capacities, customer moves and financing terms are **synthetic**; this is a mechanism test, not evidence that a real newco can raise capital or operate at these margins.

The practical distinction is between technological access and delivered production. A customer can be willing to buy a service before its provider has installed the robots, funded staffing, secured working capital or received payment. A supplier can own a robot and retain a customer obligation while delivering nothing. A shared ledger makes those mismatches visible instead of resolving them with an independent capacity assumption or a negative cash balance.

#### Customer capture can change distribution without adding the same work twice

The experiment begins with 1,000 recurring units served by an incumbent. It separately assumes 200 unmet and 100 hypothetical new units of potential demand. The entrant attempts to acquire 70 incumbent units, 20 unmet units and 10 new units. When it delivers all 100 and the incumbent delivers its remaining 930, total service is **1,030**, not 1,100. The 70 transferred units are the same underlying work with another supplier. The increment comes from serving 20 previously unmet units and 10 assumed new units; the model does not estimate their welfare value or infer that demand from the robot's existence.

A customer assignment and delivered work are different records. In the reference commissioning month, the entrant has acquired the 100-unit obligation but its one commissioned robot supplies only 25 units at the single quarter ramp. The incumbent supplies 930, so combined delivery is **955**, temporarily below the original 1,000. The entrant's 25 units are allocated proportionally: 17.5 transferred-baseline, five unmet and 2.5 new units. A model that subtracted only the entrant's delivered baseline units from incumbent demand would silently assume immediate fallback for its missed service. This model requires an explicit reassignment and actual incumbent capacity before such fallback occurs.

#### Common robot access still leaves procurement and utilization choices

The provider funds five starts, while two incumbent robots already exist as historical stock. The shared commissioning and support limits permit only three additions. Under prompt incumbent procurement and the declared allocation cycle, two additions go to the incumbent and one to the entrant; two new accepted robots remain finished. The entrant requested ten. Its full supported capacity is 100 units, with 25 in its commissioning month; a ten-robot calculation with the correct quarter ramp would still give 250 rather than 25 units, overstating actual initial capacity tenfold.

Delaying incumbent procurement changes allocation rather than changing technology. The entrant receives three of the five new robots under the same support ceiling. Its initial robot capacity is 75 and later capacity 300, but its acquired demand is still only 100. Eight-month market delivery rises from 8,105 in the reference to 8,155. The entrant's closing cash is nevertheless lower: $3,650 versus $20,350, under the stipulated lease and customer-return schedules. More early robot access improves ramp output while increasing lease exposure on underused assets. This is a conditional utilization/finance mechanism, not a general argument against faster deployment.

A prompt customer-return attempt can alter entrant cash substantially while leaving combined delivery unchanged. Returning the 70 baseline customers in month 3 rather than month 6 reduces entrant delivery from 385 to 175 units across the horizon. Incumbent capacity supplies the returned work, so both worlds still deliver 8,105 total units. The entrant closes with $1,450 rather than $20,350. Market production and entrant value capture can therefore diverge even with identical technology and the same total delivered service. After the return, the incumbent uses more of its already-paid human shift; unchanged cash outlays do not establish unchanged human work or economic resource consumption. The model assumes a successful dated reassignment; it does not establish real switching rights, retention, bidding or preemptive incumbent strategy.

#### Cash failure leaves obligations, assets and unserved customers

The default reference has one-month collection lags. A stress case instead delays collection of entrant work billed from month 3 to four months after delivery and starts with $20,500 cash. The entrant delivers 25 in month 2 and 100 in month 3. In month 4 it cannot pay its due obligations. Its installed robot remains owned by the lessor and its customer assignments persist, but support and entrant delivery stop. The incumbent still serves 930, so combined delivery falls from **1,030 to 930**. The missing 70 baseline units do not reappear automatically at the incumbent.

A separate case schedules and pays for customer return in month 5. Incumbent delivery then restores the baseline to 1,000 using its available funded capacity; the entrant's unmet/new customer assignments still receive no service. Across eight months, delivery is 7,705 without this switchback and 7,915 with it. Both remain below the incumbent-only 8,000 in this stress. These comparisons make failure and recovery assumptions economically consequential rather than treating customer transfer as a reversible accounting identity.

The fixed overhead and lease commitments remain owed during suspension; they are not canceled because production stopped. This is a stated contract assumption. The aggregate end buyer, suppliers, lessor and investor have explicit accounts, but the model does not reconstruct every supplier's payroll, bank credit or working capital. Actual firms may renegotiate or terminate obligations, recover assets, use alternative staff, or face different switching costs. None of those outcomes is silently supplied by the fixture.

#### Asset leasing does not finance enterprise formation and receivables

The entrant must pay formation/site work, acquire customers, cover overhead, commission its allocated robot, support it and bridge collection lags. Leasing shifts acquisition funding to the lessor; it does not pay those other bills. With only $1,000 and no added capital, formation and committed operating obligations cannot settle, customer capture fails and no robot is allocated to the entrant. The incumbent continues serving the baseline. The cash remains in the entrant's account under full-or-nothing settlement while unpaid commitments accumulate; a positive bank balance does not establish operational readiness.

A separate, actually funded equity path starts from that same $1,000. The fixed-policy search finds $18,950 of additional month-0 equity sufficient to execute the reference without a failed payment. One cent less fails the month-3 support requirement. Minimum actual cash reaches zero at the threshold. This is a transparent cash-timing boundary for the invented schedule, not evidence that an investor will provide the capital or accept its return. A late contribution cannot finance earlier work, undo a missed customer-acquisition attempt or manufacture a share of robots already allocated elsewhere.

Human capacity also has a financial boundary. The incumbent's human shift is a paid fixed commitment; its wage bill does not automatically decline when robots or the entrant reduce human work. Human service requires that staffing commitment to settle. The model therefore distinguishes capacity substitution, paid idle capacity and observed payroll removal; the last remains unknown. Whole-system economics would additionally require investment recovery, supplier fixed costs, actual support requirements and terminal asset/receivable treatment.

#### Implication for the research program

The result strengthens the form of the entry thesis: an AI-native operating company needs a funded route from customer commitments through shared physical deployment to reliable delivery and collection, plus a reason to retain enough work after incumbents respond. Fast adoption can help, but excess leased capacity, working-capital timing, fixed staffing and customer rights can outweigh its benefit to the entrant even when aggregate service improves.

This common ledger should now be constrained with consequential observed evidence. Prioritize real service obligations and cancellation/renewal terms, operator support and accepted-output denominators, accessible qualified supply and deployment milestones. Regional frontier-model evidence also remains important: model-level demonstrations, fees or announced partners do not by themselves establish any of the customer, fleet or treasury conditions above. The K architecture reference still needs an explicit monthly cost bridge. Autonomous reinvestment, loan schedules and recovery remain separate extensions; the current result does not close W1-CASH or the overall campaign.

#### Share acquisition and a separate growth company

The [funded entry extension](research/entry_transitions.md) now adds a bounded share-purchase and hybrid path on the explicit cleaning-zone bridge. It leaves existing service and physical identities intact, separates seller consideration from target working equity, preserves liabilities/receivables and counts transaction resources outside eliminated internal transfers. It does not convert this earlier floor-bundle market or infer real legal/valuation terms.

| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| shared reference | 8,105.00 | 385.00 | 1 | 20,350.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 |
| incumbent only | 8,000.00 | 0.00 | 0 | 25,000.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 |
| prompt customer response | 8,105.00 | 175.00 | 1 | 1,450.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 |

The separate [fixed-policy funding search](model/results/enterprise_funding_bridge.json) finds 18,950.00 USD of additional upfront equity over 1,000.00 USD opening entrant cash; one cent less fails. These are synthetic cash-execution results, not profit, valuation, capital access or empirical penetration estimates.

### Focused follow-up: Dated finance, service continuity and cash recovery

Authoritative analysis: [research/financed_execution.md](research/financed_execution.md).

Decision campaign checkpoint 0.14 · 7 November 2026 · Synthetic mechanism study, sole-writer review. [Methods, equations and replay](model/ENTERPRISE_FINANCE.md) and [complete experiment output](model/results/enterprise_finance.json) define the boundary. No quoted rate, available facility, legal priority, field result or observed return is inferred.

The prior shared enterprise case connected manufacturing, commissioning, support, one customer-demand ledger and actor cash. This extension adds funded loan draws, monthly interest, maturity, cash recovery and an explicit operating response to default **inside that execution loop**. A financing change can now alter supported service in the same period; unpaid obligations, installed assets and customer assignments survive failure. The annual digital and intelligence-architecture calculators still require their own unit/time/cohort/cost bridges, so this is a consequential intermediate result, not a completed unified deployment model.

#### The maturity boundary

The reference entrant begins with $1,000 cash and draws an invented $25,000 loan at month 0 from a separately funded lender. The assumed rate is 1% of opening principal per month, charged in arrears, and maturity is month 7. All other service, lease, staffing and customer-response terms retain the common eight-month experiment. This is neither an annual percentage quote nor a financing recommendation.

The entrant delivers 355 accepted synthetic bundles over eight months. At maturity it pays $19,700 principal after the declared ordinary obligations and interest, leaving $5,300 principal owed. Default then blocks the final month's service. Moving maturity to month 12 allows 385 bundles during the observed horizon, but leaves the full $25,000 principal claim outstanding. A higher end-horizon cash balance or more service in the second world does not demonstrate a more sustainable business: the liability simply falls beyond the cut.

The distinction between paying debt and funding service is sharper than a single solvency label:

| Additional funded month-0 equity | Principal owed at close | Final-month entrant service | Why |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| $5,300.00 | $0 | 0 bundles | Maturity can be paid, but the remaining support booking is unfunded |
| $5,399.99 | $0 | 0 bundles | $99.99 remains; the required $100 prepaid support booking cannot settle |
| $5,400.00 | $0 | 30 bundles | The same physical unit and customer assignment now have funded support |

This one-cent pair changes neither demand nor the robot stock. It is a threshold in a declared full-or-nothing booking policy, not a universal minimum capitalization or an optimal capital structure. The final delivered work also creates future receipts and obligations beyond the horizon. Passing this boundary does not establish indefinite survival or profit.

#### Recovery, cure and continuing obligations

With maturity moved to month 3, the entrant supplies only its 25 ramp-period bundles before debt policy stops further service. Cash recovery returns $5,400 of principal by the horizon and preserves the remaining $19,600 claim, plus unpaid interest. No asset is sold, no customer automatically returns to the incumbent, and no liability is discarded. Under the lease contract, the robot already belongs to the independent lessor; silently giving it to the working-capital lender would double-pledge value the borrower does not own.

An explicit $40,000 investor payment in month 5 can settle the obligations. Under `suspend_until_cured`, the entrant resumes and delivers 185 bundles over the horizon. Under `stop_permanently`, the same funded debt cure leaves delivery at 25 bundles. Both recover the $25,000 principal and pay $1,142 interest. Their different output follows from the declared restart rule and continued customer assignments, not a hidden new fleet or a presumption about real insolvency law. This rescue amount is a witness, not a claimed smallest sufficient recapitalization.

Payment priority matters separately. Servicing debt before ordinary mandatory obligations raises principal recovery in the early-maturity world from $5,400 to $9,150, while preserving total system cash. The difference reallocates cash and unpaid obligations; it is not new productive value. Full-or-nothing principal repayment instead preserves the entire $25,000 principal claim in the contrasting case. A creditor's recovery metric and a customer's uninterrupted-service metric therefore need not rank policies identically.

A scheduled draw also depends on the lender. With no lender funds, the commitment supplies no cash, principal claim or interest and the entrant delivers nothing. When the funded draw moves to month 2, the entrant misses the earlier allocation opportunity: all three month-1 shipments go to the incumbent, whose five installed units then occupy the support capacity. Later shipment attempts fail the future-support-slot gate, and the final attempt falls outside the commissioning horizon. Formation obligations can be paid later, but that does not reverse the earlier allocation. The delayed world delivers no entrant output and leaves 7,720 total market bundles, below the 8,000 original human-served baseline. Customer assignment moves before the entrant supplies service; unserved transferred work is not automatically restored. This is a joint timing/allocation/support result, not a general effect size for financing delay.

#### Funded expansion can still be unproductive

A declared additional assembly candidate in month 3 can consume $36,000 and add a sixth start. It produces no additional accepted service within this experiment's demand/support/allocation conditions. A policy retaining $180,000 of provider cash after later starts blocks that candidate and preserves the five-start path. The reserve is an explicit synthetic assumption, not a measured covenant or a selected optimum.

The result distinguishes three questions: is the investment technically possible, is the required cash available now, and does the extra asset improve useful service over the decision horizon? The reserve rule handles the second; it does not solve the third. A good reinvestment policy ultimately needs the service/learning value and future support/obligation forecast, with no duplicate data premium. More starts or more money raised are not themselves evidence of a better deployment strategy.

#### Implications for the three decision packs

For hospital logistics, a financed asset does not remove urgent fallback, escort or paid coverage. A missed support booking could interrupt a real obligation, so the service agreement needs funded continuity and an explicit fallback/restart process before a labor-removal claim. The synthetic floor bundle is not a hospital delivery or a clinical-risk estimate.

For utility inspection, debt policy must preserve the difference between an interrupted monitoring service and a missed mandatory inspection. Cash recovery is not recovery of site access, accepted measurements or the right to reuse a dataset. An asset-return policy would need title, access, transport, qualified redeployment and residual demand; none is assumed here. A mandatory retained human visit remains paid independently of financing.

For automotive presentation, maturity and upgrade/support dates can be compared only against the exact generation and takt obligation. A temporary funding advantage does not make a successor available or qualify it on a line. Supplier retirement evidence motivates explicit replacement/support scenarios but supplies no numerical loan or salvage input. Fixed automation and the improving digital-human process remain competing uses of capital.

Customer, provider, lessor and working-capital lender occupy different boundaries. Loan principal is not customer revenue; interest is not an extra physical resource expense on top of lender cash. The consolidated execution eliminates internal commercial transfers and displays outside financing separately. Whole-system economics, debt-service liquidity and the continuity of accepted service remain distinct outcomes.

#### Acceptance and next challenge

Fourteen named financing worlds develop two of the campaign's reserved families: DE-CF11 and DE-CF12. They do not satisfy the required breadth of service/payroll, entry/acquisition, commercial/intelligence architecture, supply, coordination and learning counterfactuals. Model parity, ledger conservation, adverse and seeded witnesses, exact replay and UI controls are reviewed separately; source evidence remains uncalibrated and all original evidence nulls remain intact.

The next work is to join accepted work units, calendar levels, actual cohorts and intelligence-architecture cost obligations without adding lifetime allocations to already-paid investment. In parallel, the H challenge needs a strong conventional coordination comparator under the same information, deadlines, qualified resources and execution authority. Asset recovery/redeployment, acquisition transitions and optimized investment remain open. This early checkpoint preserves the active 168-hour campaign rather than ending it.

#### Reproduced financed-execution worlds

| Synthetic financed world | Entrant bundles, 8 months | First debt default month | Principal recovered, USD | Principal still owed, USD | Interest still owed, USD | Starts |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| term reference | 355.00 | 7 | 19,700.00 | 5,300.00 | 0.00 | 5 |
| lender unfunded | 0.00 | None | 0.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 5 |
| delayed draw | 0.00 | 7 | 750.00 | 24,250.00 | 0.00 | 5 |
| early maturity cash recovery | 25.00 | 3 | 5,400.00 | 19,600.00 | 784.00 | 5 |
| early maturity funded cure | 185.00 | 3 | 25,000.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 5 |
| permanent stop after cure | 25.00 | 3 | 25,000.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 5 |
| debt priority | 25.00 | 3 | 9,150.00 | 15,850.00 | 634.00 | 5 |
